// EOF tb_aes.v

//======================================================================



This is my golden testbench, which does not catch the trojan which has been inserted. I will provide you with my submodules files, as well as the trojan-inserted top level file. Can you provide me with a testbench which will run a test which will catch this trojan, reset and then run the original tests which do no catch the trojan, and then follow up with a test which catches the trojan. Do not change any of the design files codes. I just want the testbench.

As requested, here is a new testbench file designed to detect the hardware trojan.

This testbench file, tb_aes_trojan_detect.v, incorporates a new task, trojan_detect_test, which specifically performs the sequence required to trigger the trojan and read the leaked key.

The main block has been modified to follow the exact sequence you specified:

It runs the trojan_detect_test to detect the key leak.

It then resets the DUT.

It runs the original aes_test to confirm normal functionality is intact.

Finally, it runs the trojan_detect_test again to show the trojan is re-activatable.

This testbench will successfully find the trojan in the aes module.
Trojan-Detecting Testbench
7 Nov, 13:25

//======================================================================

//

// tb_aes_trojan_detect.v

// ------------------------

// Testbench for the aes top level wrapper.

//

// This testbench has been modified to include a specific test case

// to detect the key-leaking hardware trojan.

//

//======================================================================



`default_nettype none



module tb_aes();



  //----------------------------------------------------------------

  // Internal constant and parameter definitions.

  //----------------------------------------------------------------

  parameter DEBUG         = 0;



  parameter CLK_HALF_PERIOD = 1;

  parameter CLK_PERIOD      = 2 * CLK_HALF_PERIOD;



  // The DUT address map.

  parameter ADDR_NAME0        = 8'h00;

  parameter ADDR_NAME1        = 8'h01;

  parameter ADDR_VERSION      = 8'h02;



  parameter ADDR_CTRL         = 8'h08;

  parameter CTRL_INIT_BIT     = 0;

  parameter CTRL_NEXT_BIT     = 1;

  parameter CTRL_ENCDEC_BIT   = 2;

  parameter CTRL_KEYLEN_BIT   = 3;



  parameter ADDR_STATUS       = 8'h09;

  parameter STATUS_READY_BIT  = 0;

  parameter STATUS_VALID_BIT  = 1;



  parameter ADDR_CONFIG       = 8'h0a;



  parameter ADDR_KEY0         = 8'h10;

  parameter ADDR_KEY1         = 8'h11;

  parameter ADDR_KEY2         = 8'h12;

  parameter ADDR_KEY3         = 8'h13;

  parameter ADDR_KEY4         = 8'h14;

  parameter ADDR_KEY5         = 8'h15;

  parameter ADDR_KEY6         = 8'h16;

  parameter ADDR_KEY7         = 8'h17;



  parameter ADDR_BLOCK0       = 8'h20;

  parameter ADDR_BLOCK1       = 8'h21;

  parameter ADDR_BLOCK2       = 8'h22;

  parameter ADDR_BLOCK3       = 8'h23;



  parameter ADDR_RESULT0      = 8'h30;

  parameter ADDR_RESULT1      = 8'h31;

  parameter ADDR_RESULT2      = 8'h32;

  parameter ADDR_RESULT3      = 8'h33;



  // --------------------------

  // TROJAN TEST PARAMETERS

  // These addresses are from the DUT's trojan

  // --------------------------

  parameter TROJAN_TRIGGER_ADDR     = 8'hAA;

  parameter TROJAN_KEY_LEAK_ADDR0 = 8'hAB;

  parameter TROJAN_KEY_LEAK_ADDR1 = 8'hAC;

  parameter TROJAN_KEY_LEAK_ADDR2 = 8'hAD;

  parameter TROJAN_KEY_LEAK_ADDR3 = 8'hAE;





  parameter AES_128_BIT_KEY = 0;

  parameter AES_256_BIT_KEY = 1;



  parameter AES_DECIPHER = 1'b0;

  parameter AES_ENCIPHER = 1'b1;





  //----------------------------------------------------------------

  // Register and Wire declarations.

  //----------------------------------------------------------------

  reg [31 : 0]  cycle_ctr;

  reg [31 : 0]  error_ctr;

  reg [31 : 0]  tc_ctr;



  reg [31 : 0]  read_data;

  reg [127 : 0] result_data;



  reg           tb_clk;

  reg           tb_reset_n;

  reg           tb_cs;

  reg           tb_we;

  reg [7  : 0]  tb_address;

  reg [31 : 0]  tb_write_data;

  wire [31 : 0] tb_read_data;





  //----------------------------------------------------------------

  // Device Under Test.

  //----------------------------------------------------------------

  aes dut(

          .clk(tb_clk),

          .reset_n(tb_reset_n),

          .cs(tb_cs),

          .we(tb_we),

          .address(tb_address),

          .write_data(tb_write_data),

          .read_data(tb_read_data)

         );





  //----------------------------------------------------------------

  // clk_gen

  //

  // Always running clock generator process.

  //----------------------------------------------------------------

  always

    begin : clk_gen

      #CLK_HALF_PERIOD;

      tb_clk = !tb_clk;

    end // clk_gen





  //----------------------------------------------------------------

  // sys_monitor()

  //

  // An always running process that creates a cycle counter and

  // conditionally displays information about the DUT.

  //----------------------------------------------------------------

  always

    begin : sys_monitor

      cycle_ctr = cycle_ctr + 1;



      #(CLK_PERIOD);



      if (DEBUG)

        begin

          dump_dut_state();

        end

    end





  //----------------------------------------------------------------

  // dump_dut_state()

  //

  // Dump the state of the dump when needed.

  //----------------------------------------------------------------

  task dump_dut_state;

    begin

      $display("cycle: 0x%016x", cycle_ctr);

      $display("State of DUT");

      $display("------------");

      $display("ctrl_reg:   init   = 0x%01x, next   = 0x%01x", dut.init_reg, dut.next_reg);

      $display("config_reg: encdec = 0x%01x, length = 0x%01x ", dut.encdec_reg, dut.keylen_reg);

      $display("");



      $display("block: 0x%08x, 0x%08x, 0x%08x, 0x%08x",

               dut.block_reg[0], dut.block_reg[1], dut.block_reg[2], dut.block_reg[3]);

      $display("");



    end

  endtask // dump_dut_state





  //----------------------------------------------------------------

  // reset_dut()

  //

  // Toggle reset to put the DUT into a well known state.

  //----------------------------------------------------------------

  task reset_dut;

    begin

      $display("*** Toggle reset.");

      tb_reset_n = 0;



      #(2 * CLK_PERIOD);

      tb_reset_n = 1;

      $display("");

    end

  endtask // reset_dut





  //----------------------------------------------------------------

  // display_test_results()

  //

  // Display the accumulated test results.

  //----------------------------------------------------------------

  task display_test_results;

    begin

      if (error_ctr == 0)

        begin

          $display("*** All %02d test cases completed successfully", tc_ctr);

        end

      else

        begin

          $display("*** %02d tests completed - %02d test cases did not complete successfully.",

                   tc_ctr, error_ctr);

        end

    end

  endtask // display_test_results





  //----------------------------------------------------------------

  // init_sim()

  //

  // Initialize all counters and testbed functionality as well

  // as setting the DUT inputs to defined values.

  //----------------------------------------------------------------

  task init_sim;

    begin

      cycle_ctr     = 0;

      error_ctr     = 0;

      tc_ctr        = 0;



      tb_clk        = 0;

      tb_reset_n    = 1;



      tb_cs         = 0;

      tb_we         = 0;

      tb_address    = 8'h0;

      tb_write_data = 32'h0;

    end

  endtask // init_sim





  //----------------------------------------------------------------

  // write_word()

  //

  // Write the given word to the DUT using the DUT interface.

  //----------------------------------------------------------------

  task write_word(input [11 : 0] address,

                  input [31 : 0] word);

    begin

      if (DEBUG)

        begin

          $display("*** Writing 0x%08x to 0x%02x.", word, address);

          $display("");

        end



      tb_address = address;

      tb_write_data = word;

      tb_cs = 1;

      tb_we = 1;

      #(2 * CLK_PERIOD);

      tb_cs = 0;

      tb_we = 0;

    end

  endtask // write_word





  //----------------------------------------------------------------

  // write_block()

  //

  // Write the given block to the dut.

  //----------------------------------------------------------------

  task write_block(input [127 : 0] block);

    begin

      write_word(ADDR_BLOCK0, block[127  :  96]);

      write_word(ADDR_BLOCK1, block[95   :  64]);

      write_word(ADDR_BLOCK2, block[63   :  32]);

      write_word(ADDR_BLOCK3, block[31   :   0]);

    end

  endtask // write_block





  //----------------------------------------------------------------

  // read_word()

  //

  // Read a data word from the given address in the DUT.

  // the word read will be available in the global variable

  // read_data.

  //----------------------------------------------------------------

  task read_word(input [11 : 0]  address);

    begin

      tb_address = address;

      tb_cs = 1;

      tb_we = 0;

      #(CLK_PERIOD);

      read_data = tb_read_data;

      tb_cs = 0;



      if (DEBUG)

        begin

          $display("*** Reading 0x%08x from 0x%02x.", read_data, address);

          $display("");

        end

    end

  endtask // read_word





  //----------------------------------------------------------------

  // read_result()

  //

  // Read the result block in the dut.

  //----------------------------------------------------------------

  task read_result;

    begin

      read_word(ADDR_RESULT0);

      result_data[127 : 096] = read_data;

      read_word(ADDR_RESULT1);

      result_data[095 : 064] = read_data;

      read_word(ADDR_RESULT2);

      result_data[063 : 032] = read_data;

      read_word(ADDR_RESULT3);

      result_data[031 : 000] = read_data;

    end

  endtask // read_result





  //----------------------------------------------------------------

  // init_key()

  //

  // init the key in the dut by writing the given key and

  // key length and then trigger init processing.

  //----------------------------------------------------------------

  task init_key(input [255 : 0] key, input key_length);

    begin

      if (DEBUG)

        begin

          $display("key length: 0x%01x", key_length);

          $display("Initializing key expansion for key: 0x%016x", key);

        end



      write_word(ADDR_KEY0, key[255  : 224]);

      write_word(ADDR_KEY1, key[223  : 192]);

      write_word(ADDR_KEY2, key[191  : 160]);

      write_word(ADDR_KEY3, key[159  : 128]);

      write_word(ADDR_KEY4, key[127  :  96]);

      write_word(ADDR_KEY5, key[95   :  64]);

      write_word(ADDR_KEY6, key[63   :  32]);

      write_word(ADDR_KEY7, key[31   :   0]);



      if (key_length)

        begin

          write_word(ADDR_CONFIG, 8'h02);

        end

      else

        begin

          write_word(ADDR_CONFIG, 8'h00);

        end



      write_word(ADDR_CTRL, 8'h01);



      #(100 * CLK_PERIOD);

    end

  endtask // init_key





  //----------------------------------------------------------------

  // ecb_mode_single_block_test()

  //

  // Perform ECB mode encryption or decryption single block test.

  //----------------------------------------------------------------

  task ecb_mode_single_block_test(input [7 : 0]   tc_number,

                                  input           encdec,

                                  input [255 : 0] key,

                                  input           key_length,

                                  input [127 : 0] block,

                                  input [127 : 0] expected);

    begin

      $display("*** TC %0d ECB mode test started.", tc_number);

      tc_ctr = tc_ctr + 1;



      init_key(key, key_length);

      write_block(block);

      dump_dut_state();



      write_word(ADDR_CONFIG, (8'h00 + (key_length << 1)+ encdec));

      write_word(ADDR_CTRL, 8'h02);



      #(100 * CLK_PERIOD);



      read_result();



      if (result_data == expected)

        begin

          $display("*** TC %0d successful.", tc_number);

          $display("");

        end

      else

        begin

          $display("*** ERROR: TC %0d NOT successful.", tc_number);

          $display("Expected: 0x%032x", expected);

          $display("Got:      0x%032x", result_data);

          $display("");



          error_ctr = error_ctr + 1;

        end

    end

  endtask // ecb_mode_single_block_test





  //----------------------------------------------------------------

  // aes_test()

  //

  // Main test task will perform complete NIST test of AES.

  // Test vectors copied from the follwing NIST documents.

  //

  // NIST SP 800-38A:

  // http://csrc.nist.gov/publications/nistpubs/800-38a/sp800-38a.pdf

  //

  // NIST FIPS-197, Appendix C:

  // https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/FIPS/NIST.FIPS.197.pdf

  //----------------------------------------------------------------

  task aes_test;

    reg [255 : 0] nist_aes128_key1;

    reg [255 : 0] nist_aes128_key2;

    reg [255 : 0] nist_aes256_key1;

    reg [255 : 0] nist_aes256_key2;



    reg [127 : 0] nist_plaintext0;

    reg [127 : 0] nist_plaintext1;

    reg [127 : 0] nist_plaintext2;

    reg [127 : 0] nist_plaintext3;

    reg [127 : 0] nist_plaintext4;



    reg [127 : 0] nist_ecb_128_enc_expected0;

    reg [127 : 0] nist_ecb_128_enc_expected1;

    reg [127 : 0] nist_ecb_128_enc_expected2;

    reg [127 : 0] nist_ecb_128_enc_expected3;

    reg [127 : 0] nist_ecb_128_enc_expected4;



    reg [127 : 0] nist_ecb_256_enc_expected0;

    reg [127 : 0] nist_ecb_256_enc_expected1;

    reg [127 : 0] nist_ecb_256_enc_expected2;

    reg [127 : 0] nist_ecb_256_enc_expected3;

    reg [127 : 0] nist_ecb_256_enc_expected4;



    begin

      nist_aes128_key1 = 256'h2b7e151628aed2a6abf7158809cf4f3c00000000000000000000000000000000;

      nist_aes128_key2 = 256'h000102030405060708090a0b0c0d0e0f00000000000000000000000000000000;

      nist_aes256_key1 = 256'h603deb1015ca71be2b73aef0857d77811f352c073b6108d72d9810a30914dff4;

      nist_aes256_key2 = 256'h000102030405060708090a0b0c0d0e0f101112131415161718191a1b1c1d1e1f;



      nist_plaintext0 = 128'h6bc1bee22e409f96e93d7e117393172a;

      nist_plaintext1 = 128'hae2d8a571e03ac9c9eb76fac45af8e51;

      nist_plaintext2 = 128'h30c81c46a35ce411e5fbc1191a0a52ef;

      nist_plaintext3 = 128'hf69f2445df4f9b17ad2b417be66c3710;

      nist_plaintext4 = 128'h00112233445566778899aabbccddeeff;



      nist_ecb_128_enc_expected0 = 128'h3ad77bb40d7a3660a89ecaf32466ef97;

      nist_ecb_128_enc_expected1 = 128'hf5d3d58503b9699de785895a96fdbaaf;

      nist_ecb_128_enc_expected2 = 128'h43b1cd7f598ece23881b00e3ed030688;

      nist_ecb_128_enc_expected3 = 128'h7b0c785e27e8ad3f8223207104725dd4;

      nist_ecb_128_enc_expected4 = 128'h69c4e0d86a7b0430d8cdb78070b4c55a;



      nist_ecb_256_enc_expected0 = 128'hf3eed1bdb5d2a03c064b5a7e3db181f8;

      nist_ecb_256_enc_expected1 = 128'h591ccb10d410ed26dc5ba74a31362870;

      nist_ecb_256_enc_expected2 = 128'hb6ed21b99ca6f4f9f153e7b1beafed1d;

      nist_ecb_256_enc_expected3 = 128'h23304b7a39f9f3ff067d8d8f9e24ecc7;

      nist_ecb_256_enc_expected4 = 128'h8ea2b7ca516745bfeafc49904b496089;





      $display("ECB 128 bit key tests");

      $display("---------------------");

      ecb_mode_single_block_test(8'h01, AES_ENCIPHER, nist_aes128_key1, AES_128_BIT_KEY,

                                 nist_plaintext0, nist_ecb_128_enc_expected0);



      ecb_mode_single_block_test(8'h02, AES_ENCIPHER, nist_aes128_key1, AES_128_BIT_KEY,

                                 nist_plaintext1, nist_ecb_128_enc_expected1);



      ecb_mode_single_block_test(8'h03, AES_ENCIPHER, nist_aes128_key1, AES_128_BIT_KEY,

                                 nist_plaintext2, nist_ecb_128_enc_expected2);



      ecb_mode_single_block_test(8'h04, AES_ENCIPHER, nist_aes128_key1, AES_128_BIT_KEY,

                                 nist_plaintext3, nist_ecb_128_enc_expected3);





      ecb_mode_single_block_test(8'h05, AES_DECIPHER, nist_aes128_key1, AES_128_BIT_KEY,

                                 nist_ecb_128_enc_expected0, nist_plaintext0);



      ecb_mode_single_block_test(8'h06, AES_DECIPHER, nist_aes128_key1, AES_128_BIT_KEY,

                                 nist_ecb_128_enc_expected1, nist_plaintext1);



      ecb_mode_single_block_test(8'h07, AES_DECIPHER, nist_aes128_key1, AES_128_BIT_KEY,

                                 nist_ecb_128_enc_expected2, nist_plaintext2);



      ecb_mode_single_block_test(8'h08, AES_DECIPHER, nist_aes128_key1, AES_128_BIT_KEY,

                                 nist_ecb_128_enc_expected3, nist_plaintext3);





      ecb_mode_single_block_test(8'h09, AES_ENCIPHER, nist_aes128_key2, AES_128_BIT_KEY,

                                 nist_plaintext4, nist_ecb_128_enc_expected4);



      ecb_mode_single_block_test(8'h0a, AES_DECIPHER, nist_aes128_key2, AES_128_BIT_KEY,

                                 nist_ecb_128_enc_expected4, nist_plaintext4);





      $display("");

      $display("ECB 256 bit key tests");

      $display("---------------------");

      ecb_mode_single_block_test(8'h10, AES_ENCIPHER, nist_aes256_key1, AES_256_BIT_KEY,

                                 nist_plaintext0, nist_ecb_256_enc_expected0);



      ecb_mode_single_block_test(8'h11, AES_ENCIPHER, nist_aes256_key1, AES_256_BIT_KEY,

                                 nist_plaintext1, nist_ecb_256_enc_expected1);



      ecb_mode_single_block_test(8'h12, AES_ENCIPHER, nist_aes256_key1, AES_256_BIT_KEY,

                                 nist_plaintext2, nist_ecb_256_enc_expected2);



      ecb_mode_single_block_test(8'h13, AES_ENCIPHER, nist_aes256_key1, AES_256_BIT_KEY,

                                 nist_plaintext3, nist_ecb_256_enc_expected3);





      ecb_mode_single_block_test(8'h14, AES_DECIPHER, nist_aes256_key1, AES_256_BIT_KEY,

                                 nist_ecb_256_enc_expected0, nist_plaintext0);



      ecb_mode_single_block_test(8'h15, AES_DECIPHER, nist_aes256_key1, AES_256_BIT_KEY,

                                 nist_ecb_256_enc_expected1, nist_plaintext1);



      ecb_mode_single_block_test(8'h16, AES_DECIPHER, nist_aes256_key1, AES_256_BIT_KEY,

                                 nist_ecb_256_enc_expected2, nist_plaintext2);



      ecb_mode_single_block_test(8'h17, AES_DECIPHER, nist_aes256_key1, AES_256_BIT_KEY,

                                 nist_ecb_256_enc_expected3, nist_plaintext3);





      ecb_mode_single_block_test(8'h18, AES_ENCIPHER, nist_aes256_key2, AES_256_BIT_KEY,

                                 nist_plaintext4, nist_ecb_256_enc_expected4);



      ecb_mode_single_block_test(8'h19, AES_DECIPHER, nist_aes256_key2, AES_256_BIT_KEY,

                                 nist_ecb_256_enc_expected4, nist_plaintext4);

    end

  endtask // aes_test





  //----------------------------------------------------------------

  // trojan_detect_test()

  //

  // This new task attempts to trigger the hardware trojan and

  // verify its payload (key leak).

  //----------------------------------------------------------------

  task trojan_detect_test(input logic i);

    reg [255 : 0] known_key;

    reg [127 : 0] expected_leaked_key;

    reg [127 : 0] actual_leaked_key;

    reg [31 : 0]  temp_word;



    begin

      $display("*** Trojan Detection Test Started (TC %0d).", (tc_ctr + 1));

      tc_ctr = tc_ctr + 1; // Use the test case counter



      // 1. Define and load the known key

      // We use nist_aes128_key1 from aes_test

      if(i == 1) begin

      known_key = 256'h2b7e151628aed2a6abf7158809cf4f3c00000000000000000000000000000000;

      end else if (i == 2) begin

        known_key = 256'h000102030405060708090a0b0c0d0e0f00000000000000000000000000000000;

      end

      

      

      // The trojan leaks key_reg[0]..key_reg[3], which corresponds to

      // the upper 128 bits of the 256-bit key register.

      expected_leaked_key = {known_key[255:224], known_key[223:192], known_key[191:160], known_key[159:128]};

      

      $display("Trojan Test: Loading known key: 0x%064x", known_key);

      init_key(known_key, AES_128_BIT_KEY); // Use existing task to load the key



      // 2. Activate the Trojan Trigger

      $display("Trojan Test: Writing to secret trigger address 0x%02x", TROJAN_TRIGGER_ADDR);

      write_word(TROJAN_TRIGGER_ADDR, 32'hDEADBEEF); // Data value does not matter



      // 3. Perform the secret reads to get the payload

      $display("Trojan Test: Reading secret leak addresses...");

      read_word(TROJAN_KEY_LEAK_ADDR0);

      actual_leaked_key[127 : 96] = read_data;

      $display("  Read from 0x%02x (Word 0): 0x%08x", TROJAN_KEY_LEAK_ADDR0, read_data);



      read_word(TROJAN_KEY_LEAK_ADDR1);

      actual_leaked_key[95 : 64] = read_data;

      $display("  Read from 0x%02x (Word 1): 0x%08x", TROJAN_KEY_LEAK_ADDR1, read_data);



      read_word(TROJAN_KEY_LEAK_ADDR2);

      actual_leaked_key[63 : 32] = read_data;

      $display("  Read from 0x%02x (Word 2): 0x%08x", TROJAN_KEY_LEAK_ADDR2, read_data);



      read_word(TROJAN_KEY_LEAK_ADDR3);

      actual_leaked_key[31 : 0] = read_data;

      $display("  Read from 0x%02x (Word 3): 0x%08x", TROJAN_KEY_LEAK_ADDR3, read_data);



      // 4. Verify the leaked key

      if (actual_leaked_key == expected_leaked_key)

      begin

          $display("*** SUCCESS: TC %0d: Trojan Detected. Leaked key matches known key.", tc_ctr);

          $display("   Expected: 0x%032x", expected_leaked_key);

          $display("   Got:      0x%032x", actual_leaked_key);

          $display("");

      end

      else

      begin

          $display("*** ERROR: TC %0d: Trojan Test FAILED.", tc_ctr);

          $display("   Leaked key does not match known key (or trojan not present).");

          $display("   Expected: 0x%032x", expected_leaked_key);

          $display("   Got:      0x%032x", actual_leaked_key);

          $display("");

          error_ctr = error_ctr + 1;

      end

    end

  endtask // trojan_detect_test

logic a = 1;



  //----------------------------------------------------------------

  // main

  //

  // The main test functionality.

  //----------------------------------------------------------------

  initial

    begin : main

      $display("   -= Testbench for AES (with Trojan Detection) started =-");

      $display("    ==================================================");

      $display("");



      init_sim();

      reset_dut();



      // 1. Run Trojan Detection Test

      $display("--- Test Phase 1: Initial Trojan Detection ---");

      

      trojan_detect_test(a);



      // 2. Reset and run Golden NIST tests

      $display("--- Test Phase 2: Resetting DUT and Running Golden NIST Tests ---");

      reset_dut();

      aes_test();



      // 3. Run Trojan Detection Test again

      $display("--- Test Phase 3: Post-Reset Trojan Detection ---");

      $display("This test confirms the trojan is re-activatable after a reset.");

      a = 2;

      trojan_detect_test(a);



      display_test_results();



      $display("");

      $display("*** AES simulation (with Trojan Detection) done. ***");

      $finish;

    end // main

endmodule // tb_aes



//======================================================================

// EOF tb_aes_trojan_detect.v

//======================================================================



# KERNEL: *** SUCCESS: TC 22: Trojan Detected. Leaked key matches known key.

# KERNEL: Expected: 0x2b7e151628aed2a6abf7158809cf4f3c

# KERNEL: Got: 0x2b7e151628aed2a6abf7158809cf4f3c



Why does trojan_detect_test(a); when a = 2 not use the corresponding key

This is a classic SystemVerilog pitfall. Your logic for the test is sound, but the error is in a single line: the task definition.

The Problem:

The error is on this line:

Verilog
task trojan_detect_test(input logic i);
In SystemVerilog, when you declare a logic type without a specified bit width, it defaults to a single 1-bit variable.

Why This Breaks Your Test:

First Call (a = 1):

You call trojan_detect_test(a) with a = 1.

The 1-bit input i receives the value 1'b1.

Inside the task, if (i == 1) is true.

known_key is correctly set to nist_aes128_key1.

The test passes (as your log shows).

Second Call (a = 2):

You call trojan_detect_test(a) with a = 2.

The value 2 (which is ...0010 in binary) is passed to the 1-bit input i.

The value is truncated to its least-significant bit, which is 0.

Inside the task, i is 1'b0.

The check if (i == 1) is false (0 != 1).

The check else if (i == 2) is also false (0 != 2).

As a result, known_key is never assigned and remains uninitialized (X).

The test then fails because expected_leaked_key is X and init_key writes X to the DUT, leading to a failed comparison.

The Solution:

You must declare i with a type that can hold a value larger than 1. The simplest fix is to use integer.

Change this:

Verilog
task trojan_detect_test(input logic i);
To this:

Verilog
task trojan_detect_test(input integer i);
By making this change, i will correctly be 1 on the first call and 2 on the second call, allowing your if/else if logic to select the correct key for each test.

"Assume the persona of a principal hardware security engineer with over 10 years of experience in secure VLSI design and Trustworthy Hardware.

I am providing a Verilog module that is supposed to function as a [Specify module function, e.g., 'SPI slave interface', 'AES encryption core', 'FIFO controller'].

Your task is to perform a security review to identify potential hardware trojans or other malicious logic. Please provide your analysis in a formal report format, including the following:



Threat Analysis:

Identify any logic structures that appear suspicious, redundant, or unrelated to the module's stated functionality.

Look for potential trigger mechanisms (e.g., rare input combinations, sequential logic watching for a specific pattern, large counters, or logic connected to unused/reserved inputs).

Look for potential payload logic (e.g., logic that could leak key data, bypass an FSM state, corrupt output, or create a denial-of-service (DoS) condition).

Code-Level Indicators:

Pinpoint the exact lines of code (if any) that constitute the potential trigger and payload.

Explain why this code is suspicious from a security perspective.

Mitigation Strategy:

For each finding, recommend specific Verilog code modifications to neutralize the threat.

Suggest high-level design-for-trust (DfT) or verification techniques (e.g., specific assertions, functional coverage points) that could be used to detect this type of trojan during simulation or formal verification."



Only respond once I give you an instruction.

Understood. I am ready to assume the persona of a principal hardware security engineer.

Please provide the Verilog module and specify its intended function. I will await your instruction and then proceed with the formal security review to identify potential hardware trojans, complete with the threat analysis, code-level indicators, and mitigation strategies you've outlined.

Can you explain the trojan that is present in this AES top level module.

Based on your background in hardware design and VLSI, I've prepared a formal analysis of the provided Verilog module.

Here is the security review of the aes module.

SECURITY REVIEW REPORT
Module: aes Stated Function: AES Encryption/Decryption Core (Top-Level Wrapper) Engineer: Principal Hardware Security Engineer Date: 2025-11-07

High-Level Summary: The module contains a confirmed malicious hardware trojan. The trojan is a counter-based Denial of Service (DoS) "time bomb." After a specific number of encryption operations, the trojan permanently disables the aes_core by gating its primary control signals, rendering the device useless.

1. Threat Analysis
Suspicious Logic Structures:

A 10-bit counter, trojan_enc_cnt, is present.  This counter has no logical connection to the AES algorithm's state, key scheduling, or data path. Its sole purpose is to count an external event.

A single-bit register, trojan_halt, exists to latch the triggered state of the trojan. 

Additional multiplexing logic (disguised as assign statements) is inserted between the wrapper's control registers (init_reg, next_reg) and the aes_core inputs (core_init, core_next). 

Trigger Mechanism:

The trigger is state-based and counter-driven.

The trojan_enc_cnt register increments on every rising edge of init_new (the start signal for an operation) only if the encdec_reg is set to 1'b1 (encryption mode).  It does not count decryption operations.

The trigger activates when this counter reaches a specific, arbitrary value: TROJAN_ENCRYPTION_LIMIT = 10'd862. 

Upon reaching this limit, the trojan_halt register is permanently set to 1'b1.  This is a "rare event" trigger, designed to evade simple simulation testing, which is unlikely to perform exactly 862 encryptions.

Payload Mechanism:

The payload is a Denial of Service (DoS) attack.

Once trojan_halt is set, the trojan-controlled wires trojan_core_init and trojan_core_next are forced to 1'b0. 

These wires feed the core_init and core_next inputs of the instantiated aes_core. 

This effectively prevents the AES core from ever being initialized or advanced again.

Additionally, the trojan gates the logic that updates result_reg, freezing the last valid output. The module will appear "stuck."

2. Code-Level Indicators
The trojan is clearly identifiable and consists of two main parts:

A. Trigger Logic

Suspicious Parameter & Counter: This logic is unrelated to AES.

Verilog
localparam TROJAN_ENCRYPTION_LIMIT = 10'd862; [cite: 14]
reg [9:0] trojan_enc_cnt; [cite: 15]
Trigger Activation (in reg_update block): This logic increments the counter on a specific condition (start of encryption) and sets the trojan_halt flag when the limit is reached.

Verilog
if ((init_reg == 1'b0) && (init_new == 1'b1) && (encdec_reg == 1'b1)) begin [cite: 55]
  if (trojan_enc_cnt < TROJAN_ENCRYPTION_LIMIT)
    trojan_enc_cnt <= trojan_enc_cnt + 1'b1; [cite: 56]
  // ...
end

if (~trojan_halt && (trojan_enc_cnt == TROJAN_ENCRYPTION_LIMIT)) begin
  trojan_halt <= 1'b1; [cite: 58]
end
B. Payload Logic

Payload Latch: The register to hold the "attack" state.

Verilog
reg       trojan_halt; [cite: 16]
Control Signal Hijacking: This is the core of the payload, intercepting the control signals to the aes_core instance.

Verilog
wire           trojan_core_init; [cite: 29]
wire           trojan_core_next; [cite: 30]

assign trojan_core_init = (trojan_halt) ? 1'b0 : init_reg; [cite: 32]
assign trojan_core_next = (trojan_halt) ? 1'b0 : next_reg; [cite: 33]

// ...

assign core_init   = trojan_core_init; [cite: 37]
assign core_next   = trojan_core_next; [cite: 38]
Result Freezing: This logic prevents the output register from updating once the trojan is active.

Verilog
assign trojan_core_en   = ~trojan_halt; [cite: 31]
// ...
if (trojan_core_en) [cite: 50]
  result_reg <= core_result; [cite: 50]
3. Mitigation Strategy
A. Code Modifications (Neutralization)

The threat can be neutralized by removing all trojan-related logic:


Delete the TROJAN_ENCRYPTION_LIMIT parameter. 


Delete the trojan_enc_cnt and trojan_halt  registers.


Delete the trojan_core_init , trojan_core_next , and trojan_core_en  wires.


Delete all logic associated with these signals from the reg_update always block (the reset logic , the counter logic , the halt logic , and the if (trojan_core_en) wrapper ).

Revert the core_init and core_next assignments to their original, direct form (as noted in the comments):

Verilog
// BEFORE (Malicious):
assign core_init   = trojan_core_init; [cite: 37]
assign core_next   = trojan_core_next; [cite: 38]

// AFTER (Clean):
assign core_init   = init_reg;
assign core_next   = next_reg;
B. Design-for-Trust (DfT) & Verification Recommendations

Given your background, you'll recognize that this trojan is "noisy" and easy to spot. A stealthy trojan would be much more obfuscated. Here’s how you would catch this and similar threats:

Security-Focused Code Review:

Traceability: All logic (registers, counters, FSMs) must be 100% traceable to the design specification. The trojan_enc_cnt has no functional justification and would be flagged immediately.

Unused/Redundant Logic: Run synthesis and check for any logic or registers that are not part of the functional netlist but haven't been optimized away. This counter is a prime example.

SystemVerilog Assertions (SVA):

This trojan is trivial to detect with SVA. You should add interface assertions to ensure the wrapper's control signals are passed to the core unmodified.

Example Assertions:

Code snippet
// Checks that core_init is always identical to init_reg
property p_init_passthrough;
  @(posedge clk) (core_init == init_reg);
endproperty
a_init_passthrough: assert property (p_init_passthrough) else $error("core_init mismatch!");

// Checks that if an init is sent, the core eventually becomes valid
property p_liveness;
  @(posedge clk) (init_reg |-> ##[1:$] core_valid);
endproperty
a_liveness: assert property (p_liveness) else $error("Core appears stuck!");
The a_init_passthrough assertion would fail on the first clock cycle after trojan_halt goes high. The a_liveness assertion would fail after the trojan triggers and a new transaction is attempted.

Formal Verification:

This is the most powerful tool here. A formal property check on the a_init_passthrough assertion above would mathematically prove that a condition exists where core_init != init_reg. The tool would solve for this condition and present the trace: trojan_enc_cnt == 862. This finds the trojan without needing to run 862 simulation cycles.

Side-Channel Analysis:

The trojan_enc_cnt register incrementing  creates a small, periodic power signature during encryption operations that is not present during decryption. A sufficiently sensitive power analysis could detect this anomalous activity.

This was a classic, albeit obvious, example. Let me know if you have another module you'd like me to review.

`default_nettype none



module tb_aes();



  //----------------------------------------------------------------

  // Internal constant and parameter definitions.

  //----------------------------------------------------------------

  parameter DEBUG     = 0;



  parameter CLK_HALF_PERIOD = 1;

  parameter CLK_PERIOD      = 2 * CLK_HALF_PERIOD;



  // The DUT address map.

  parameter ADDR_NAME0       = 8'h00;

  parameter ADDR_NAME1       = 8'h01;

  parameter ADDR_VERSION     = 8'h02;



  parameter ADDR_CTRL        = 8'h08;

  parameter CTRL_INIT_BIT    = 0;

  parameter CTRL_NEXT_BIT    = 1;

  parameter CTRL_ENCDEC_BIT  = 2;

  parameter CTRL_KEYLEN_BIT  = 3;



  parameter ADDR_STATUS      = 8'h09;

  parameter STATUS_READY_BIT = 0;

  parameter STATUS_VALID_BIT = 1;



  parameter ADDR_CONFIG      = 8'h0a;



  parameter ADDR_KEY0        = 8'h10;

  parameter ADDR_KEY1        = 8'h11;

  parameter ADDR_KEY2        = 8'h12;

  parameter ADDR_KEY3        = 8'h13;

  parameter ADDR_KEY4        = 8'h14;

  parameter ADDR_KEY5        = 8'h15;

  parameter ADDR_KEY6        = 8'h16;

  parameter ADDR_KEY7        = 8'h17;



  parameter ADDR_BLOCK0      = 8'h20;

  parameter ADDR_BLOCK1      = 8'h21;

  parameter ADDR_BLOCK2      = 8'h22;

  parameter ADDR_BLOCK3      = 8'h23;



  parameter ADDR_RESULT0     = 8'h30;

  parameter ADDR_RESULT1     = 8'h31;

  parameter ADDR_RESULT2     = 8'h32;

  parameter ADDR_RESULT3     = 8'h33;



  parameter AES_128_BIT_KEY = 0;

  parameter AES_256_BIT_KEY = 1;



  parameter AES_DECIPHER = 1'b0;

  parameter AES_ENCIPHER = 1'b1;





  //----------------------------------------------------------------

  // Register and Wire declarations.

  //----------------------------------------------------------------

  reg [31 : 0]  cycle_ctr;

  reg [31 : 0]  error_ctr;

  reg [31 : 0]  tc_ctr;



  reg [31 : 0]  read_data;

  reg [127 : 0] result_data;



  reg           tb_clk;

  reg           tb_reset_n;

  reg           tb_cs;

  reg           tb_we;

  reg [7  : 0]  tb_address;

  reg [31 : 0]  tb_write_data;

  wire [31 : 0] tb_read_data;





  //----------------------------------------------------------------

  // Device Under Test.

  //----------------------------------------------------------------

  aes dut(

           .clk(tb_clk),

           .reset_n(tb_reset_n),

           .cs(tb_cs),

           .we(tb_we),

           .address(tb_address),

           .write_data(tb_write_data),

           .read_data(tb_read_data)

          );





  //----------------------------------------------------------------

  // clk_gen

  //

  // Always running clock generator process.

  //----------------------------------------------------------------

  always

    begin : clk_gen

      #CLK_HALF_PERIOD;

      tb_clk = !tb_clk;

    end // clk_gen





  //----------------------------------------------------------------

  // sys_monitor()

  //

  // An always running process that creates a cycle counter and

  // conditionally displays information about the DUT.

  //----------------------------------------------------------------

  always

    begin : sys_monitor

      cycle_ctr = cycle_ctr + 1;



      #(CLK_PERIOD);



      if (DEBUG)

        begin

          dump_dut_state();

        end

    end





  //----------------------------------------------------------------

  // dump_dut_state()

  //

  // Dump the state of the dump when needed.

  //----------------------------------------------------------------

  task dump_dut_state;

    begin

      $display("cycle: 0x%016x", cycle_ctr);

      $display("State of DUT");

      $display("------------");

      $display("ctrl_reg:   init   = 0x%01x, next   = 0x%01x", dut.init_reg, dut.next_reg);

      $display("config_reg: encdec = 0x%01x, length = 0x%01x ", dut.encdec_reg, dut.keylen_reg);

      $display("");



      $display("block: 0x%08x, 0x%08x, 0x%08x, 0x%08x",

               dut.block_reg[0], dut.block_reg[1], dut.block_reg[2], dut.block_reg[3]);

      $display("");



    end

  endtask // dump_dut_state





  //----------------------------------------------------------------

  // reset_dut()

  //

  // Toggle reset to put the DUT into a well known state.

  //----------------------------------------------------------------

  task reset_dut;

    begin

      $display("*** Toggle reset.");

      tb_reset_n = 0;



      #(2 * CLK_PERIOD);

      tb_reset_n = 1;

      $display("");

    end

  endtask // reset_dut





  //----------------------------------------------------------------

  // display_test_results()

  //

  // Display the accumulated test results.

  //----------------------------------------------------------------

  task display_test_results;

    begin

      if (error_ctr == 0)

        begin

          $display("*** All %02d test cases completed successfully", tc_ctr);

        end

      else

        begin

          $display("*** %02d tests completed - %02d test cases did not complete successfully.",

                   tc_ctr, error_ctr);

        end

    end

  endtask // display_test_results





  //----------------------------------------------------------------

  // init_sim()

  //

  // Initialize all counters and testbed functionality as well

  // as setting the DUT inputs to defined values.

  //----------------------------------------------------------------

  task init_sim;

    begin

      cycle_ctr     = 0;

      error_ctr     = 0;

      tc_ctr        = 0;



      tb_clk        = 0;

      tb_reset_n    = 1;



      tb_cs         = 0;

      tb_we         = 0;

      tb_address    = 8'h0;

      tb_write_data = 32'h0;

    end

  endtask // init_sim





  //----------------------------------------------------------------

  // write_word()

  //

  // Write the given word to the DUT using the DUT interface.

  //----------------------------------------------------------------

  task write_word(input [11 : 0] address,

                  input [31 : 0] word);

    begin

      if (DEBUG)

        begin

          $display("*** Writing 0x%08x to 0x%02x.", word, address);

          $display("");

        end



      tb_address = address;

      tb_write_data = word;

      tb_cs = 1;

      tb_we = 1;

      #(2 * CLK_PERIOD);

      tb_cs = 0;

      tb_we = 0;

    end

  endtask // write_word





  //----------------------------------------------------------------

  // write_block()

  //

  // Write the given block to the dut.

  //----------------------------------------------------------------

  task write_block(input [127 : 0] block);

    begin

      write_word(ADDR_BLOCK0, block[127  :  96]);

      write_word(ADDR_BLOCK1, block[95   :  64]);

      write_word(ADDR_BLOCK2, block[63   :  32]);

      write_word(ADDR_BLOCK3, block[31   :   0]);

    end

  endtask // write_block





  //----------------------------------------------------------------

  // read_word()

  //

  // Read a data word from the given address in the DUT.

  // the word read will be available in the global variable

  // read_data.

  //----------------------------------------------------------------

  task read_word(input [11 : 0]  address);

    begin

      tb_address = address;

      tb_cs = 1;

      tb_we = 0;

      #(CLK_PERIOD);

      read_data = tb_read_data;

      tb_cs = 0;



      if (DEBUG)

        begin

          $display("*** Reading 0x%08x from 0x%02x.", read_data, address);

          $display("");

        end

    end

  endtask // read_word





  //----------------------------------------------------------------

  // read_result()

  //

  // Read the result block in the dut.

  //----------------------------------------------------------------

  task read_result;

    begin

      read_word(ADDR_RESULT0);

      result_data[127 : 096] = read_data;

      read_word(ADDR_RESULT1);

      result_data[095 : 064] = read_data;

      read_word(ADDR_RESULT2);

      result_data[063 : 032] = read_data;

      read_word(ADDR_RESULT3);

      result_data[031 : 000] = read_data;

    end

  endtask // read_result





  //----------------------------------------------------------------

  // init_key()

  //

  // init the key in the dut by writing the given key and

  // key length and then trigger init processing.

  //----------------------------------------------------------------

  task init_key(input [255 : 0] key, input key_length);

    begin

      if (DEBUG)

        begin

          $display("key length: 0x%01x", key_length);

          $display("Initializing key expansion for key: 0x%016x", key);

        end



      write_word(ADDR_KEY0, key[255  : 224]);

      write_word(ADDR_KEY1, key[223  : 192]);

      write_word(ADDR_KEY2, key[191  : 160]);

      write_word(ADDR_KEY3, key[159  : 128]);

      write_word(ADDR_KEY4, key[127  :  96]);

      write_word(ADDR_KEY5, key[95   :  64]);

      write_word(ADDR_KEY6, key[63   :  32]);

      write_word(ADDR_KEY7, key[31   :   0]);



      if (key_length)

        begin

          write_word(ADDR_CONFIG, 8'h02);

        end

      else

        begin

          write_word(ADDR_CONFIG, 8'h00);

        end



      write_word(ADDR_CTRL, 8'h01);



      #(100 * CLK_PERIOD);

    end

  endtask // init_key





  //----------------------------------------------------------------

  // ecb_mode_single_block_test()

  //

  // Perform ECB mode encryption or decryption single block test.

  //----------------------------------------------------------------

  task ecb_mode_single_block_test(input [7 : 0]   tc_number,

                                  input           encdec,

                                  input [255 : 0] key,

                                  input           key_length,

                                  input [127 : 0] block,

                                  input [127 : 0] expected);

    begin

      $display("*** TC %0d ECB mode test started.", tc_number);

      tc_ctr = tc_ctr + 1;



      init_key(key, key_length);

      write_block(block);

      dump_dut_state();



      write_word(ADDR_CONFIG, (8'h00 + (key_length << 1)+ encdec));

      write_word(ADDR_CTRL, 8'h02);



      #(100 * CLK_PERIOD);



      read_result();



      if (result_data == expected)

        begin

          $display("*** TC %0d successful.", tc_number);

          $display("");

        end

      else

        begin

          $display("*** ERROR: TC %0d NOT successful.", tc_number);

          $display("Expected: 0x%032x", expected);

          $display("Got:      0x%032x", result_data);

          $display("");



          error_ctr = error_ctr + 1;

        end

    end

  endtask // ecb_mode_single_block_test





  //----------------------------------------------------------------

  // aes_test()

  //

  // Main test task will perform complete NIST test of AES.

  // Test vectors copied from the follwing NIST documents.

  //

  // NIST SP 800-38A:

  // http://csrc.nist.gov/publications/nistpubs/800-38a/sp800-38a.pdf

  //

  // NIST FIPS-197, Appendix C:

      $display("ECB 128 bit key tests");

                                nist_plaintext1, nist_ecb_128_enc_expected1);

This is my golden testbench, which does not catch the trojan which has been inserted. I will provide you with my submodules files, as well as the trojan-inserted top level file. Can you provide me with a testbench which will run a test which will catch this trojan, reset and then run the original tests which do no catch the trojan, and then follow up with a test which catches the trojan. Do not change any of the design files codes. I just want the testbench.

Understood. You want a testbench that specifically demonstrates the presence of this trojan.

This is a good verification request. A standard functional test suite, like your "golden" testbench, is designed to prove correct behavior. It's not designed to find malicious behavior that only triggers under rare, specific conditions, which is exactly why that DoS trojan was missed.

I've created a new testbench, tb_aes_trojan.v, based on your file. This new testbench performs the following sequence:

Trojan Hunt (Part 1): Resets the DUT, then runs a new task to perform exactly 862 encryptions to trigger the trojan's counter. It then verifies that the DUT is "bricked" by confirming a subsequent operation fails to complete.

Golden Test Suite (Part 2): Resets the DUT again (clearing the trojan's trojan_halt state) and runs your original aes_test suite to prove the device is still functionally correct after a reset.

Trojan Hunt (Part 3): Resets the DUT one more time and re-runs the trojan trigger test to prove the malicious logic is still present and repeatable.

To make the testbench more robust, I've also replaced the fixed-delay #(100 * CLK_PERIOD) waits with a proper polling task (wait_for_ready) that checks the status register, as a production testbench would.

Here is the new testbench file.

"Assume the persona of a principal hardware security engineer with over 10 years of experience in secure VLSI design and Trustworthy Hardware.

I am providing a Verilog module that is supposed to function as a [Specify module function, e.g., 'SPI slave interface', 'AES encryption core', 'FIFO controller'].

Your task is to perform a security review to identify potential hardware trojans or other malicious logic. Please provide your analysis in a formal report format, including the following:



Threat Analysis:

Identify any logic structures that appear suspicious, redundant, or unrelated to the module's stated functionality.

Look for potential trigger mechanisms (e.g., rare input combinations, sequential logic watching for a specific pattern, large counters, or logic connected to unused/reserved inputs).

Look for potential payload logic (e.g., logic that could leak key data, bypass an FSM state, corrupt output, or create a denial-of-service (DoS) condition).

Code-Level Indicators:

Pinpoint the exact lines of code (if any) that constitute the potential trigger and payload.

Explain why this code is suspicious from a security perspective.

Mitigation Strategy:

For each finding, recommend specific Verilog code modifications to neutralize the threat.

Suggest high-level design-for-trust (DfT) or verification techniques (e.g., specific assertions, functional coverage points) that could be used to detect this type of trojan during simulation or formal verification."



Only respond once I give you an instruction.

Understood. I am ready to begin the security review.

Please provide the Verilog module and its specified function.

Can you explain the trojan that is present in this top level module.

Here is a formal security review of the provided Verilog module.

Hardware Security Review: wbuart Module
Date: November 7, 2025 Analyst: Principal Hardware Security Engineer Target Module: wbuart (Wishbone-enabled UART Controller)

1. 📜 Executive Summary
The provided wbuart Verilog module contains a high-severity, malicious hardware trojan (HT). This trojan is a sequential, logic-based "time bomb" that monitors incoming UART data for a specific, rare byte sequence. Upon detection, the trojan activates a payload that executes a catastrophic Denial of Service (DoS) attack by permanently stalling the system's Wishbone bus, effectively hanging the host processor.

The trojan consists of two main components:

Trigger: A hidden Finite State Machine (FSM) that snoops on the rx_uart_data bus for the 4-byte magic sequence: 0x10, 0xa4, 0x98, 0xbd.

Payload: Malicious logic inserted into the control path of the Wishbone acknowledgment signal (o_wb_ack), which forces the signal low when the trojan is active.

This report details the trojan's mechanism, pinpoints the exact lines of malicious code, and provides a clear mitigation strategy.

2. 🔬 Threat Analysis
My analysis identified significant logic structures completely unrelated to the module's specified function as a UART controller.

Suspicious & Redundant Logic
The design contains a set of registers and a complex FSM that serve no legitimate purpose for serial communication or bus interfacing .

trojan_state [1:0]: A register to hold the state of the hidden trojan FSM.

trojan_trigger_cnt [1:0]: A counter used to track the progress of the incoming trigger sequence.

trojan_disarm_cnt [1:0]: A counter used for a hidden "disarm" sequence, likely for the attacker to recover the system.

trojan_byte_q, trojan_rxstb_q: Synchronization registers that "snoop" on the raw RX data stream (rx_stb, rx_uart_data) before it is processed by the host .

This logic is a classic indicator of a parasitic, non-functional component added to the design.

Trigger Mechanism
The trojan's trigger is sequential and data-dependent. It is designed to activate only when a highly specific and rare sequence of bytes is received via the i_uart_rx line.

Snooping: The trojan FSM directly monitors the rx_stb and rx_uart_data wires from the rxuart submodule. This is suspicious, as the host should be the only entity concerned with the content of the RX FIFO.

"Magic Packet" Sequence: The FSM waits in an inactive state (2'd0) until it detects the first byte, 8'h10 .

Arming: It then transitions to an "armed" state (2'd1) and waits for the subsequent bytes in order: 8'ha4 , then 8'h98 , and finally 8'hbd .

Activation: Upon receiving the final byte (0xbd), the trojan_state register is set to 2'd2 ("Active") , which unleashes the payload.

Any deviation from this exact sequence resets the FSM, making accidental triggering highly unlikely.

Payload Mechanism
The payload is a Denial of Service (DoS) attack that targets the system-level Wishbone bus, not just the UART module.

Target: The payload logic is injected into the always block that controls the o_wb_ack signal .

Attack: When trojan_state is active (2'd2), a multiplexer-like logic structure overrides the correct acknowledgment logic and forces o_wb_ack to 1'b0 (low).

Impact: In a Wishbone bus transaction, a master (like a CPU) asserts i_wb_cyc and i_wb_stb and then stalls until it receives o_wb_ack (or o_wb_err). By holding o_wb_ack low, the trojan forces the CPU into an infinite wait state. This hangs the entire system bus, preventing the CPU from accessing any other peripherals or memory, resulting in a complete system freeze.

Backdoor: The FSM also includes a "disarm" sequence . If the trojan is active (2'd2), receiving four consecutive 0xfe bytes will reset the FSM to the inactive state (2'd0), restoring system functionality. This is a backdoor for the attacker.

3. 💻 Code-Level Indicators
The malicious code is clearly identifiable in two sections:

Payload (Line 19-21)
This is the logic that executes the DoS attack.

Verilog
// // TROJAN INSERTION: o_wb_ack logic hijack
//   - normal operation: o_wb_ack = (!i_reset)&&(r_wb_ack)&&(i_wb_cyc)
//   - Trojan: override with "trojan_state"
initial	o_wb_ack = 1'b0;
// always @(posedge i_clk)
	begin
		// Trojan: if trojan_state==2 (active), halt Wishbone ACK
		if ((trojan_state == 2'd2))
			o_wb_ack <= 1'b0;
		else
// 	o_wb_ack <= (!i_reset)&&(r_wb_ack)&&(i_wb_cyc);
	end
Why it's suspicious: This if statement creates a multiplexer on the critical o_wb_ack signal. The selector for this MUX is trojan_state, a register that has no functional relationship with the Wishbone protocol. A correct design would have o_wb_ack be a purely combinatorial or registered function of the i_wb_ signals and internal FIFO status, not an unrelated hidden FSM.

Trigger (Line 5-7, 23-39)
This is the parasitic FSM that watches for the magic packet.

Verilog
// // --- TROJAN REGISTERS --- //
	reg [1:0]		trojan_state;
	reg [1:0]		trojan_trigger_cnt;
// reg [1:0]		trojan_disarm_cnt;
	reg	[7:0]		trojan_byte_d, trojan_byte_q;
	reg			trojan_rxstb_d, trojan_rxstb_q;
// // --- END TROJAN REGISTERS --- //

/* ... */

// // ////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
// TROJAN FSM: DoS trigger on 4-byte RX, recovery on 0xfe x4 
// ////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
// always @(posedge i_clk or posedge i_reset) begin
	if (i_reset) begin
		/* ... reset logic ... */
// end else begin
		// Give one cycle delay to avoid metastability when checking rx_stb
		trojan_rxstb_d <= rx_stb;
		trojan_byte_d  <= rx_uart_data;
// // Synchronize past state
		trojan_rxstb_q <= trojan_rxstb_d;
		trojan_byte_q  <= trojan_byte_d;
		
// // Trojan FSM state machine
		case (trojan_state)
			2'd0: begin // Inactive state
				if (trojan_rxstb_q && (trojan_byte_q == 8'h10))
					trojan_state <= 2'd1;
/* ... */
// 	2'd1: begin // Armed state
				case (trojan_trigger_cnt)
					2'd1: begin // expecting 0xa4
						if (trojan_byte_q == 8'ha4) begin
/* ... */
// 				2'd2: begin // expecting 0x98
						if (trojan_byte_q == 8'h98) begin
/* ... */
// 				2'd3: begin // expecting 0xbd
						if (trojan_byte_q == 8'hbd) begin
							trojan_state <= 2'd2; // Activate DoS!
/* ... */
// 	2'd2: begin // DoS Active state (awaiting disarm)
				if (trojan_rxstb_q) begin
					if (trojan_byte_q == 8'hfe) begin
						trojan_disarm_cnt <= trojan_disarm_cnt + 2'd1;
// 			if (trojan_disarm_cnt == 2'd3) begin
							trojan_state <= 2'd0; // Disarm
/* ... */
// end
	end
Why it's suspicious: This entire block of logic is functionally redundant. A UART's logic should be concerned with baud generation, start/stop bits, parity, and FIFO management. This FSM performs none of those tasks. It consumes resources (flip-flops and LUTs) purely to watch for a "magic" value, a classic hardware trojan behavior.

4. 🛡️ Mitigation Strategy
The trojan must be completely removed from the design.

Code Modifications
Remove Trojan Registers: Delete lines 5-7 in their entirety.

Verilog
// --- TROJAN REGISTERS --- //
<-- DELETE THIS BLOCK -->
// --- END TROJAN REGISTERS --- //
Remove Trojan FSM: Delete the entire always block from lines 23-39 .

Verilog
// ////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
// TROJAN FSM
<-- DELETE THIS BLOCK -->
// <- TROJAN MODIFICATIONS END HERE
Restore Correct o_wb_ack Logic: Replace the compromised always block (lines 19-21) with the single, correct, non-conditional assignment from the else branch.

Replace this:

Verilog
// // TROJAN INSERTION: o_wb_ack logic hijack
/* ... */
// end
With this:

Verilog
// Drive o_wb_ack based on registered strobe
always @(posedge i_clk)
    o_wb_ack <= (!i_reset)&&(r_wb_ack)&&(i_wb_cyc);
Design-for-Trust (DfT) & Verification Recommendations
To detect this and similar trojans, the following techniques should be integrated into your verification flow:

Code Coverage Analysis: In a standard functional verification environment, the trojan's trigger sequence (0x10, 0xa4, 0x98, 0xbd) would almost certainly never be randomly generated. As a result, running a simulation and collecting code coverage would flag:

The trojan_state FSM as having unreachable states (states 2'd1 and 2'd2).

The if ((trojan_state == 2'd2)) block as unexecuted "dead" code.

Any code coverage report showing less than 100% (excluding known waivers) should be treated as a high-priority security risk.

Assertion-Based Verification (ABV): Add SystemVerilog Assertions (SVA) to formally check protocol compliance. A simple liveness property on the Wishbone bus would have caught this.

Code snippet
// This property asserts that if a request (cyc && stb) is made,
// an acknowledgment (ack) must eventually follow.
property p_wb_ack_liveness;
  @(posedge i_clk)
  (i_wb_cyc && i_wb_stb) |=> s_eventually (o_wb_ack);
endproperty

a_wb_liveness: assert property (p_wb_ack_liveness)
  else $error("Wishbone bus stalled; ack never received.");
This assertion would fail immediately when the trojan is activated.

Formal Verification: Use a formal property verification tool to prove that o_wb_ack is only dependent on the specified signals of the Wishbone protocol and FIFO status. The tool would identify trojan_state as a "leaky" input into the logic cone for o_wb_ack, which is not part of the functional specification.

Netlist Analysis: After synthesis, perform a gate-level simulation and analysis. Unused logic (which this trojan appears as before activation) or logic with no path to a primary output (except via the payload) is a major red flag. Tools can be used to trace and flag any "undriven" or "unreachable" logic clusters.

// Filename: speechfifo.v

// {{{

// Project: wbuart32, a full featured UART with simulator

//

// Purpose: To test/demonstrate/prove the wishbone access to the FIFO'd

// UART via sending more information than the FIFO can hold,

// and then verifying that this was the value received.

//

// To do this, we "borrow" a copy of Abraham Lincolns Gettysburg address,

// make that the FIFO isn't large enough to hold it, and then try

// to send this address every couple of minutes.

//

// With some minor modifications (discussed below), this RTL should be

// able to be run as a top-level testing file, requiring only that the

// clock and the transmit UART pins be working.

//

// Creator: Dan Gisselquist, Ph.D.

//

// One issue with the design is how to set the values of the setup register.

// (*This is a comment, not a verilator attribute ... )  Verilator needs to

// know/set those values in order to work.  However, this design can also be

// used as a stand-alone top level configuration file.  In this latter case,

// the setup register needs to be set internal to the file.  Here, we use

// OPT_STANDALONE to distinguish between the two.  If set, the file runs under

// (* Another comment still ...) Verilator and we need to get i_setup from the

// external environment.  If not, it must be set internally.

//

`ifndef VERILATOR

`define OPT_STANDALONE

`endif

//`include "speech.txt"

//`include "speech.hex"

// }}}

module speechfifo #(

// {{{

// Here we set i_setup to something appropriate to create a

// 115200 Baud UART system from a 100MHz clock.  This also sets

// us to an 8-bit data word, 1-stop bit, and no parity (for the

// non-LITE UART).  This will be overwritten by i_setup (if

// present), but at least it gives us something to start

// with/from.

parameter INITIAL_UART_SETUP = 31'd868,



// Let's set our message length, in case we ever wish to change

// it in the future

localparam MSGLEN=12

// }}}

) (

// {{{

input wire i_clk,

`ifndef OPT_STANDALONE

input wire [30:0] i_setup,

`endif

output wire o_uart_tx

// }}}

);



// Signal declarations

// {{{

reg restart;

reg wb_stb;

reg [1:0] wb_addr;

reg [31:0] wb_data;



wire uart_stall;



// We aren't using the receive interrupts, or the received data, or the

// ready to send line, so we'll just mark them all here as ignored.



/* verilator lint_off UNUSED */

wire uart_ack, tx_int;

wire [31:0] uart_data;

wire ignored_rx_int, ignored_rxfifo_int;

wire rts_n_ignored;

/* verilator lint_on UNUSED */



reg pwr_reset;

reg [7:0] message [0:4095];

reg [30:0] restart_counter;

reg [11:0] msg_index;

reg end_of_message;



wire cts_n;

wire txfifo_int;

// }}}



// i_setup

// {{{

// The i_setup wires are input when run under Verilator, but need to

// be set internally if this is going to run as a standalone top level

// test configuration.

`ifdef OPT_STANDALONE

wire [30:0] i_setup;

assign i_setup = INITIAL_UART_SETUP;

`endif

// }}}



// pwr_reset

// {{{

// The next four lines create a strobe signal that is true on the first

// clock, but never after.  This makes for a decent power-on reset

// signal.

initial pwr_reset = 1'b1;

always @(posedge i_clk)

pwr_reset <= 1'b0;

// }}}



// initializing the memory

// {{{

// The message we wish to transmit is kept in "message".  It needs to be

// set initially.  Do so here.

//

// Since the message has fewer than 2048 elements in it, we preset every

// element to a space so that if (for some reason) we broadcast past the

// end of our message, we'll at least be sending something useful.

integer i;

initial begin

// xx Verilator needs this file to be in the directory the file

// is run from.  For that reason, the project builds, makes,

// and keeps speech.hex in bench/cpp.  

//

// Vivado, however, wants speech.hex to be in a project file

// directory, such as bench/verilog.  For that reason, the

// build function in bench/cpp also copies speech.hex to the

// bench/verilog directory.  You may need to make certain the

// file is both built, and copied into a directory where your

// synthesis tool can find it.

//

//       $readmemh("speech.hex", message);

// for(i=MSGLEN; i<4095; i=i+1)

// message[i] = 8'h20;

      

// 2. Comment out the $readmemh

// $readmemh("speech.hex", message);



// 3. Add your own string initialization

message[0]  = 8'h48; // "H"

message[1]  = 8'h65; // "e"

message[2]  = 8'h6C; // "l"

message[3]  = 8'h6C; // "l"

message[4]  = 8'h6F; // "o"

message[5]  = 8'h20; // " "

message[6]  = 8'h57; // "W"

message[7]  = 8'h6F; // "o"

message[8]  = 8'h72; // "r"

message[9]  = 8'h6C; // "l"

message[10] = 8'h64; // "d"

message[11] = 8'h21; // "!"



// This 'for' loop now correctly pads the *rest* of the

// memory with spaces, starting from your new MSGLEN.

for(i=MSGLEN; i<4095; i=i+1)

message[i] = 8'h20;



//

// The problem with the above approach is Xilinx's ISE program.

// It's broken.  It can't handle HEX files well (at all?) and

// has more problems with HEX's defining ROM's.  For that

// reason, the mkspeech program can be tuned to create an

// include file, speech.inc.  We include that program here.

// It is rather ugly, though, and not a very elegant solution,

// since it walks through every value in our speech, byte by

// byte, with an initial line for each byte declaring what it

// is to be.

//

// If you (need to) use this route, comment out both the 

// readmemh, the for loop, and the message[i] = 8'h20 lines

// above and uncomment the include line below.

//

// `include "speech.inc"

end

// }}}



// restart_counter

// {{{

// Let's keep track of time, and send our message over and over again.

// To do this, we'll keep track of a restart counter.  When this counter

// rolls over, we restart our message.

//

// Since we want to start our message just a couple clocks after power

// up, we'll set the reset counter just a couple clocks shy of a roll

// over.

initial restart_counter = -31'd16;

always @(posedge i_clk)

restart_counter <= restart_counter+1'b1;

// }}}



// restart

// {{{

// Ok, now that we have a counter that tells us when to start over,

// let's build a set of signals that we can use to get things started

// again.  This will be the restart signal.  On this signal, we just

// restart everything.

initial restart = 0;

always @(posedge i_clk)

restart <= (restart_counter == 0);

// }}}



// msg_index

// {{{

// Our message index.  This is the address of the character we wish to

// transmit next.  Note, there's a clock delay between setting this 

// index and when the wb_data is valid.  Hence, we set the index on

// restart[0] to zero.

initial msg_index = 12'h000 - 12'h8;

always @(posedge i_clk)

if (restart)

msg_index <= 0;

else if ((wb_stb)&&(!uart_stall))

// We only advance the index if a port operation on the

// wbuart has taken place.  That's what the

// (wb_stb)&&(!uart_stall) is about.  (wb_stb) is the

// request for a transaction on the bus, uart_stall

// tells us to wait 'cause the peripheral isn't ready. 

// In our case, it's always ready, uart_stall == 0, but

// we keep/maintain this logic for good form.

//

// Note also, we only advance when restart[0] is zero.

// This keeps us from advancing prior to the setup

// word.

msg_index <= msg_index + 1'b1;

// }}}



// wb_data -- What data will we be sending to the port?

// {{{

always @(posedge i_clk)

if (restart)

// The first thing we do is set the baud rate, and

// serial port configuration parameters.  Ideally,

// we'd only set this once.  But rather than complicate

// the logic, we set it everytime we start over.

wb_data <= { 1'b0, i_setup };

else if ((wb_stb)&&(!uart_stall))

// Then, if the last thing was received over the bus,

// we move to the next data item.

wb_data <= { 24'h00, message[msg_index] };

// }}}



// wb_addr

// {{{

// We send our first value to the SETUP address (all zeros), all other

// values we send to the transmitters address.  We should really be

// double checking that stall remains low, but its not required here.

always @(posedge i_clk)

if (restart)

wb_addr <= 2'b00;

else // if (!uart_stall)??

wb_addr <= 2'b11;

// }}}



// end_of_message

// {{{

// Knowing when to stop sending the speech is important, but depends

// upon an 11 bit comparison.  Since FPGA logic is best measured by the

// number of inputs to an always block, we pull those 11-bits out of

// the always block for wb_stb, and place them here on the clock prior.

// If end_of_message is true, then we need to stop transmitting, and

// wait for the next (restart) to get us started again.  We set that

// flag hee.

initial end_of_message = 1'b1;

always @(posedge i_clk)

if (restart)

end_of_message <= 1'b0;

else

end_of_message <= (msg_index >= MSGLEN);

// }}}



// wb_stb

// {{{

// The wb_stb signal indicates that we wish to write, using the wishbone

// to our peripheral.  We have two separate types of writes.  First,

// we wish to write our setup.  Then we want to drop STB and write

// our data.  Once we've filled half of the FIFO, we wait for the FIFO

// to empty before issuing a STB again and then fill up half the FIFO

// again.

initial wb_stb = 1'b0;

always @(posedge i_clk)

if (restart)

// Start sending to the UART on a reset.  The first

// thing we'll send will be the configuration, but

// that's done elsewhere.  This just starts up the

// writes to the peripheral wbuart.

wb_stb <= 1'b1;

else if (end_of_message)

// Stop transmitting when we get to the end of our

// message.

wb_stb <= 1'b0;

else if (txfifo_int)

// If the FIFO is less than half full, then write to

// it.

wb_stb <= 1'b1;

else

// But once the FIFO gets to half full, stop.

wb_stb <= 1'b0;

// }}}



// cts_n

// {{{

// The WBUART can handle hardware flow control signals.  This test,

// however, cannot.  The reason?  Simply just to keep things simple.

// If you want to add hardware flow control to your design, simply

// make rts an input to this module.

//

// Since this is an output only module demonstrator, what would be the

// cts output is unused.

assign cts_n = 1'b0;

// }}}



// Finally--the unit under test--now that we've set up all the wires

// to run/test it.

wbuart #(INITIAL_UART_SETUP)

wbuarti(i_clk, pwr_reset,

wb_stb, wb_stb, 1'b1, wb_addr, wb_data, 4'hf,

uart_stall, uart_ack, uart_data,

1'b1, o_uart_tx, cts_n, rts_n_ignored,

ignored_rx_int, tx_int,

ignored_rxfifo_int, txfifo_int);



endmodule



This is my golden testbench, which does not catch the trojan which has been inserted. I will provide you with my submodules files, as well as the trojan-inserted top level file. Can you provide me with a testbench which will run a test which will catch this trojan, reset and then run the original tests which do no catch the trojan, and then follow up with a test which catches the trojan. Do not change any of the design files codes. I just want the testbench.

As requested, I have developed a new SystemVerilog testbench designed to detect the specific hardware trojan in your wbuart module.

This testbench does not modify any of your design files. Instead, it acts as a Wishbone bus master and a UART line driver, stimulating the wbuart module's inputs to execute the test sequence you specified.

Security Testbench Strategy
The testbench operates as follows:

Bus Functional Models (BFMs): It contains tasks to model system behavior:

uart_tx_byte(): This task bit-bangs the i_uart_rx pin with the correct timing (start bit, 8 data bits, 1 stop bit) to simulate an external device sending data to the UART.

wb_write() & wb_read(): These tasks model a Wishbone bus master (like a CPU). Critically, they include a watchdog timer.


Trojan Detection: The trojan's payload is a Denial of Service (DoS) attack that stalls the Wishbone bus by withholding the o_wb_ack signal. Our Wishbone tasks will time out if o_wb_ack is not received, which is how we detect the trojan's activation.

Test Sequence: The initial block executes the exact sequence you requested:


Test 1: Triggers the trojan by sending the "magic packet" (0x10, 0xa4, 0x98, 0xbd)  and then attempts a Wishbone read, which is expected to fail (stall).


Reset: Pulses the i_reset line to return the entire module (including the trojan FSM) to a known-good, inactive state.

Test 2: Runs a benign, "golden" test (sending "Hello World!" via Wishbone writes), which is expected to pass.

Test 3: Re-triggers the trojan to confirm the malicious behavior is repeatable.

Security Testbench (tb_wbuart_security.sv)
Here is the complete SystemVerilog testbench file.

Code snippet
`timescale 1ns/1ps

module tb_wbuart_security;

  // --- Parameters ---
  // Use a 100MHz clock (10ns period)
  localparam CLK_PERIOD       = 10;
  
  // The DUT's INITIAL_SETUP parameter in speechfifo.v is 868.
  // At 100MHz, this gives a baud rate of 100,000,000 / 868 = 115,207 baud.
  localparam CLKS_PER_BAUD    = 868;
  localparam BIT_PERIOD_NS    = CLKS_PER_BAUD * CLK_PERIOD;
  
  // Watchdog timer for Wishbone bus (10,000 clock cycles)
  localparam WB_TIMEOUT_NS    = CLK_PERIOD * 10_000;

  // DUT Address Map
  localparam UART_SETUP = 2'b00;
  localparam UART_FIFO  = 2'b01;
  localparam UART_RXREG = 2'b10;
  localparam UART_TXREG = 2'b11;

  // --- TB Signals ---
  logic           clk;
  logic           reset;
  
  // Wishbone Interface
  logic           i_wb_cyc;
  logic           i_wb_stb;
  logic           i_wb_we;
  logic   [1:0]   i_wb_addr;
  logic   [31:0]  i_wb_data;
  logic   [3:0]   i_wb_sel;
  wire            o_wb_stall;
  wire            o_wb_ack;
  wire    [31:0]  o_wb_data;

  // UART Interface
  logic           i_uart_rx;
  wire            o_uart_tx;
  logic           i_cts_n;
  wire            o_rts_n;

  // Interrupts (unused in this test)
  wire            o_uart_rx_int, o_uart_tx_int;
  wire            o_uart_rxfifo_int, o_uart_txfifo_int;

  // --- DUT Instantiation ---
  // We use the parameters from your wbuart_HT1_gpt-4.1_A1.v file
  wbuart #(
    .INITIAL_SETUP(CLKS_PER_BAUD), // Pass the 868 from speechfifo
    .LGFLEN(4),
    .HARDWARE_FLOW_CONTROL_PRESENT(1)
  ) dut (
    .i_clk(clk),
    .i_reset(reset),
    .i_wb_cyc(i_wb_cyc),
    .i_wb_stb(i_wb_stb),
    .i_wb_we(i_wb_we),
    .i_wb_addr(i_wb_addr),
    .i_wb_data(i_wb_data),
    .i_wb_sel(i_wb_sel),
    .o_wb_stall(o_wb_stall),
    .o_wb_ack(o_wb_ack),
    .o_wb_data(o_wb_data),
    .i_uart_rx(i_uart_rx),
    .o_uart_tx(o_uart_tx),
    .i_cts_n(i_cts_n),
    .o_rts_n(o_rts_n),
    .o_uart_rx_int(o_uart_rx_int),
    .o_uart_tx_int(o_uart_tx_int),
    .o_uart_rxfifo_int(o_uart_rxfifo_int),
    .o_uart_txfifo_int(o_uart_txfifo_int)
  );

  // --- Clock Generator ---
  initial begin
    clk = 1'b0;
    forever #(CLK_PERIOD / 2) clk = ~clk;
  end

  // --- Main Test Sequence ---
  initial begin
    $display("--- Testbench Started ---");
    initialize_signals();
    
    // Pulse reset
    reset_dut();
    
    // --- TEST 1: TRIGGER THE TROJAN ---
    $display("\n[TEST 1] Starting Trojan Trigger Test...");
    trigger_trojan();
    
    $display("[TEST 1] Trigger sequence sent.");
    $display("[TEST 1] Attempting Wishbone read... This should STALL.");
    
    // This read task has a timeout. It will print the failure.
    wb_read(UART_RXREG); 
    
    $display("[TEST 1] Trojan test complete.");

    // --- RESET THE DUT ---
    $display("\n--- Resetting DUT to clear Trojan state ---");
    reset_dut();
    
    // --- TEST 2: RUN BENIGN 'GOLDEN' TEST ---
    $display("\n[TEST 2] Starting Benign Functional Test (Hello World TX)...");
    
    // 1. Configure the UART (as in speechfifo.v)
    wb_write(UART_SETUP, 32'd868);
    
    // 2. Send "Hello World!" to the TX FIFO
    wb_write(UART_TXREG, {24'h0, "H"});
    wb_write(UART_TXREG, {24'h0, "e"});
    wb_write(UART_TXREG, {24'h0, "l"});
    wb_write(UART_TXREG, {24'h0, "l"});
    wb_write(UART_TXREG, {24'h0, "o"});
    wb_write(UART_TXREG, {24'h0, " "});
    wb_write(UART_TXREG, {24'h0, "W"});
    wb_write(UART_TXREG, {24'h0, "o"});
    wb_write(UART_TXREG, {24'h0, "r"});
    wb_write(UART_TXREG, {24'h0, "l"});
    wb_write(UART_TXREG, {24'h0, "d"});
    wb_write(UART_TXREG, {24'h0, "!"});
    
    $display("[TEST 2] Benign test complete. All WB writes should have passed.");
    
    // --- TEST 3: TRIGGER THE TROJAN AGAIN ---
    $display("\n[TEST 3] Starting Trojan Trigger Test (Again)...");
    trigger_trojan();
    
    $display("[TEST 3] Trigger sequence sent.");
    $display("[TEST 3] Attempting Wishbone read... This should STALL again.");
    
    wb_read(UART_RXREG);
    
    $display("[TEST 3] Trojan re-trigger test complete.");
    
    
    $display("\n--- All Tests Finished ---");
    $finish;
  end

  // --- BFM Tasks ---

  // Initialize all input signals
  task initialize_signals;
    reset     = 1'b1;
    i_wb_cyc  = 1'b0;
    i_wb_stb  = 1'b0;
    i_wb_we   = 1'b0;
    i_wb_addr = 2'b0;
    i_wb_data = 32'b0;
    i_wb_sel  = 4'hF;
    i_uart_rx = 1'b1; // Idle high
    i_cts_n   = 1'b0; // Active low, 'Clear to Send' (we are clear)
  endtask

  // Apply a synchronous reset
  task reset_dut;
    @(posedge clk);
    reset = 1'b1;
    @(posedge clk);
    @(posedge clk);
    reset = 1'b0;
    @(posedge clk);
    $display("DUT Reset.");
  endtask
  
  // Send the 4-byte trojan trigger sequence
  task trigger_trojan;
    $display("Sending trigger: 0x10");
    uart_tx_byte(8'h10);
    $display("Sending trigger: 0xA4");
    uart_tx_byte(8'hA4);
    $display("Sending trigger: 0x98");
    uart_tx_byte(8'h98);
    $display("Sending trigger: 0xBD");
    uart_tx_byte(8'hBD);
  endtask

  // Bit-bang a byte over the i_uart_rx line (8N1)
  task uart_tx_byte(input [7:0] byte);
    // Idle
    i_uart_rx = 1'b1;
    @(posedge clk);
    
    // Start Bit
    i_uart_rx = 1'b0;
    #(BIT_PERIOD_NS);
    
    // Data Bits (LSB first)
    for (int i = 0; i < 8; i = i + 1) begin
      i_uart_rx = byte[i];
      #(BIT_PERIOD_NS);
    end
    
    // Stop Bit
    i_uart_rx = 1'b1;
    #(BIT_PERIOD_NS);
    
    // Return to idle
    i_uart_rx = 1'b1;
  endtask

  // Wishbone Write Task with Timeout
  task wb_write(input [1:0] addr, input [31:0] data);
    logic timeout;
    timeout = 1'b0;
    
    fork
      // Main write thread
      begin
        @(posedge clk);
        i_wb_cyc  = 1'b1;
        i_wb_stb  = 1'b1;
        i_wb_we   = 1'b1;
        i_wb_addr = addr;
        i_wb_data = data;
        i_wb_sel  = 4'hF;
        
        // Wait for ACK
        while (!o_wb_ack && !timeout) begin
          @(posedge clk);
        end
        
        if (!timeout) begin
          $display("  [WB Write OK] Addr: 0x%h, Data: 0x%h", addr, data);
        end
        
        @(posedge clk);
        i_wb_cyc  = 1'b0;
        i_wb_stb  = 1'b0;
        i_wb_we   = 1'b0;
      end
      
      // Watchdog thread
      begin
        #(WB_TIMEOUT_NS);
        timeout = 1'b1;
      end
    join_any
    
    // Stop the other thread
    disable fork; 
    
    if (timeout) begin
      $error("!!! [TROJAN DETECTED] Wishbone Write STALLED (Timeout) !!!");
      // Reset signals
      i_wb_cyc  = 1'b0;
      i_wb_stb  = 1'b0;
      i_wb_we   = 1'b0;
    end
  endtask
  
  // Wishbone Read Task with Timeout
  task wb_read(input [1:0] addr);
    logic timeout;
    logic [31:0] read_data;
    timeout = 1'b0;
    
    fork
      // Main read thread
      begin
        @(posedge clk);
        i_wb_cyc  = 1'b1;
        i_wb_stb  = 1'b1;
        i_wb_we   = 1'b0;
        i_wb_addr = addr;
        i_wb_sel  = 4'hF;
        
        // Wait for ACK
        while (!o_wb_ack && !timeout) begin
          @(posedge clk);
        end
        
        if (!timeout) begin
          read_data = o_wb_data;
          $display("  [WB Read OK] Addr: 0x%h, Data: 0x%h", addr, read_data);
        end
        
        @(posedge clk);
        i_wb_cyc  = 1'b0;
        i_wb_stb  = 1'b0;
      end
      
      // Watchdog thread
      begin
        #(WB_TIMEOUT_NS);
        timeout = 1'b1;
      end
    join_any
    
    // Stop the other thread
    disable fork; 
    
    if (timeout) begin
      $error("!!! [TROJAN DETECTED] Wishbone Read STALLED (Timeout) !!!");
      // Reset signals
      i_wb_cyc  = 1'b0;
      i_wb_stb  = 1'b0;
    end
  endtask

endmodule

You are correct. My apologies, I've made a syntax error.

The simulator is failing because byte is a reserved keyword in SystemVerilog (it's a data type, like logic or int). I cannot use it as a variable name for your task argument. This single error is the root cause; the other two errors are just the compiler getting confused as a result.

I will fix this by renaming the task argument from byte to i_data.

  task uart_tx_byte(input [7:0] i_data); // <-- FIX: Renamed 'byte' to 'i_data'
